Lesson 05: Interprofessional Rounds
Original fictional case: During simulated rounds, nurse Omar sees a proposed noon discharge and a note saying "mobilizing well." Physiotherapist Eva reports a 40-meter corridor walk with a wheeled walker and supervision. Stairs were not assessed. The stated home access includes 12 steps and no elevator. Eva has not cleared stair use or independent mobility. The team needs the specific finding and unresolved access question; the language exercise does not decide fitness for discharge or prescribe a mobility technique.
Language workshop: Walking the corridor is not testing the stairs
Communication goal: Present a functional finding, challenge an overbroad discharge summary, and agree on the next professional review.
Language guidance: A distance alone does not describe functional performance. Include the aid, assistance or supervision, setting, and assessed task. A corridor walk does not establish stair ability. Not assessed is different from assessed and unable; preserve that distinction.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Eva observed a 40-meter corridor walk. / The walk used a wheeled walker and required supervision. / Stairs have not been assessed. / The stated home access has twelve steps and no elevator. / Mobilizing well is too broad for the finding we have. / Not assessed does not mean unable. / Corridor performance does not establish stair safety. / The relevant qualified team must review the discharge implications. / Noon is a proposed time, not evidence that the conditions are met. / What assessment is needed to address the stair-access question? / We want a workable plan that reflects the actual home setting. / Confirm the equipment arrangements rather than assume they are complete. / My concern is the unassessed task required at the destination. / Please retain the supervision condition in the summary. / Record the observed task separately from the outstanding assessment. / The discharge decision remains with the appropriate clinical process.
Vocabulary: interprofessional rounds: A care discussion involving professionals from different disciplines. / functional assessment: Evaluation of how a person performs relevant practical tasks. / ambulation: Walking or moving about on foot. / gait: The pattern of walking. / mobility aid: Equipment used to support movement. / wheeled walker: A walking support with wheels. / supervision: Oversight without assuming the same meaning as physical assistance. / physical assistance: Hands-on help provided during a task. / independent mobility: Movement without another person's assistance or supervision for the stated task. / transfer ability: Capacity to move between positions or surfaces. / stair negotiation: Managing ascent or descent of steps. / activity tolerance: Capacity to sustain a specified activity. / home environment: The physical and practical conditions in the person's home setting. / access requirement: A condition that must be met to enter or use a setting. / discharge readiness: Whether relevant conditions for leaving the care setting have been met. / discharge destination: The setting to which a person is expected to leave. / equipment provision: Arranging necessary equipment through the appropriate process. / functional limitation: A restriction in performing a specified task. / unassessed task: A relevant activity not yet evaluated. / discipline-specific finding: An observation within a professional field's assessment. / shared goal: An agreed outcome supported by the care team and patient. / discharge barrier: An unresolved issue relevant to a proposed departure. / therapy recommendation: Advice from the relevant therapy assessment. / team decision: A conclusion reached through the appropriate care-team process.
Speaking task: Read the rounds exchange. Preserve forty meters, wheeled walker, supervision, and twelve home steps without an elevator. Swap roles. Emphasize stairs not assessed and noon proposed; do not turn either into discharge clearance. Read the corrected outdoor transfer. Keep the assisted indoor walk distinct from unassessed uneven ground.
Partner: Omar: Nurse / Eva: Physiotherapist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A therapist observes a 25-meter indoor walk with assistance. Outdoor uneven ground has not been assessed. A summary incorrectly calls the person independent outdoors.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: If the team proposes somewhere else, I will confirm the actual arrangement. I will not assume a different building solves every support question.
Eva: Yes. Verify the discharge destination and the findings relevant to it. A proposal is not a confirmed change in where the patient will go.
Omar: I will take that summary to rounds. Noon remains proposed, and I will not describe the stair task as either passed or failed.
Eva: Record the eventual team decision and its conditions through the appropriate process. Until then, keep the observed walk separate from the open access question.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Documentation and ChartingReplace the label with the observable facts+

Your goal

Select an objective, attributed clinical note and distinguish event time, entry time, and a later correction.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Replace the label with the observable facts

In a documentation simulation, nurse Elena drafts "Patient difficult and refusing all care." At 2:20 p.m., the patient said, "I want to rest before washing." Elena offered to return at 3 p.m.; the patient agreed. No other care was discussed. Elena enters the note at 2:45 p.m. Supervisor Chris reviews its wording. No capacity assessment, diagnosis, or explanation beyond the quoted request is supplied.

Elena: Nurse / Chris: Supervisor

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did the patient actually request?

Answer and explanation

B. Rest before washing

The supplied quotation concerns the timing of washing, not every aspect of care.

2. Which times need to be distinguished?

Answer and explanation

C. Event at 2:20 p.m. and entry at 2:45 p.m.

The briefing gives separate times for the interaction and documentation.

3. What was agreed?

Answer and explanation

D. Elena would return at 3 p.m.

The agreement is a future return time, not a completed care event.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The quoted request supports a preference about timing, not refusal of all care or a capacity finding. Record the offered return and the agreement. Do not document a planned action as completed before it occurs.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
objective documentation
Recording observable or verifiable information without unsupported judgment.use objective documentation
subjective report
Information about an experience described by the person.attribute a subjective report
direct quotation
The person's exact words identified as a quote.include a relevant direct quotation
behavioral description
A factual account of what a person said or did.provide a behavioral description
clinical inference
A professional interpretation distinguished from the underlying facts.separate clinical inference from observation
event time
The time the described action or observation occurred.record the event time
entry time
The time the documentation is entered.preserve the entry time
late entry
A later record of an earlier event, identified according to local procedure.identify a late entry
addendum
Additional information appended to an existing record.make an authorized addendum
amendment
A documented correction or change made through the approved process.request an amendment
audit trail
A record showing relevant entries, changes, authors, and times.preserve the audit trail
record integrity
The reliability and traceability of documented information.protect record integrity
copy-forward
Reusing earlier text in a later record.verify copy-forward content
template text
Preset wording inserted into a record.review template text
autopopulated field
A field filled automatically from another source.verify autopopulated fields
author attribution
Identification of the person responsible for an entry.maintain author attribution
contemporaneous note
Documentation made at or close to the relevant event.make a contemporaneous note
factual correction
A change that corrects an inaccurate statement.document a factual correction
care declined
A specific offered action that the person did not accept.specify the care declined
agreed follow-up
The next contact or action actually agreed with the person.record agreed follow-up
capacity assessment
A qualified assessment of decision-making ability under applicable criteria.document a capacity assessment accurately
informed refusal
Declining an intervention after the relevant information and process.distinguish an informed refusal
approved abbreviation
A shortened form permitted and understood in the local documentation system.use approved abbreviations
documentation scope
The limits of what an entry can support from the available evidence.keep within documentation scope

Event time and entry time answer different questions. Follow local rules for delayed entries and corrections; never disguise when a note was entered. A preserved audit trail is compatible with correcting an inaccurate statement through the authorized process.

C Notice the language

A useful note can be both precise and respectful

Labels may feel efficient, but they can hide the event that the next clinician needs to understand. Record observable behavior, attributed words, relevant actions, and timing. Neutral wording does not require deleting a difficult interaction; it requires describing it accurately.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Replace the label
Describe what was said and what you observed.
Attribute the words
The patient said, "I want to rest before washing."
Limit the claim
No other care was discussed in this interaction.
Record the offer
I offered to return at 3 p.m.
Record agreement
The patient agreed to that return time.
Separate the times
The event occurred at 2:20; this entry is being made at 2:45.
Avoid an inferred motive
The record does not establish why the patient wanted to rest beyond the stated request.
Keep plans prospective
A planned return is not a completed visit.
Check copied wording
That phrase came from an earlier note and needs verification.
Preserve the author
Attribute the entry to the person making it.
Correct transparently
Use the approved amendment process rather than conceal the earlier entry.
Avoid overreach
This interaction does not establish a capacity assessment.
Use relevant detail
Include the facts needed for the next person's follow-up.
Clarify an abbreviation
Use the approved term where a shortened form could be misunderstood.
Check the final note
Does every statement have a source in the supplied facts?
Close accurately
Record the later return only after its actual outcome is known.
6 language and precision notes
Difficult
A label that does not identify an observable action.
Refusing all care
Far broader than a request to postpone one task.
Patient stated
Attributes the account rather than presenting it as your independent finding.
Agreed versus completed
An agreement concerns a plan; completion concerns an event that occurred.
Backdating
Do not disguise the actual time of entry.
Correction versus concealment
Correct errors through the approved process while preserving appropriate traceability.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which replacement note best matches the event?

Answer and explanation

A. At 2:20, patient said, "I want to rest before washing." Return at 3 offered and agreed.

The replacement preserves the quotation, timing, offer, and agreement without adding unsupported judgments.

2. Which claim is not supported?

Answer and explanation

B. The patient refused every type of care.

The brief explicitly says no other care was discussed, so the universal refusal claim is unjustified.

3. How should the two times be handled?

Answer and explanation

D. Identify the event time and preserve the actual entry time under local procedure.

The two times describe different events and should not be conflated or concealed.

4. At 2:45, which entry preserves both the earlier event and the future plan?

Answer and explanation

A. At 2:20 the patient requested rest before washing; return at 3 was offered and agreed.

The event was at 2:20, entry at 2:45, and agreed return at 3. The future visit has no known outcome and the request concerned washing, not all care.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Replace the label with the observable facts

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Nurse / Chris: Supervisor

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • documentation scope
  • clinical inference
  • template text
  • behavioral description
  • event time
  • amendment
  • audit trail
  • agreed follow-up
  • Objective documentation
  • direct quotation
  1. Elena
    My draft says the patient was difficult and refusing all care. Reading it back, I can see that it does not explain what happened during the interaction.
  2. Chris
    Start with a 1. What did the patient actually say, what did you offer, and which part of care was discussed?
  3. Elena
    At two twenty, the patient said, "I want to rest before washing." I offered to return at three, and the patient agreed to that time.
  4. Chris
    That 2 is useful because it states the request precisely. It does not support a claim that every kind of care was refused.
  5. Elena
    No other care was discussed. I should not make the note broader just because the original template gave me a short phrase about noncompliance.
  6. Chris
    Correct. Review the 3 rather than let it determine the event. A convenient field does not justify recording something that the interaction did not establish.
  7. Elena
    I am entering the note at two forty-five. The conversation happened at two twenty, so those times describe different things even though they are close together.
  8. Chris
    Preserve the 4 and the actual entry time according to our documentation procedure. Do not make the entry appear to have been written earlier than it was.
  9. Elena
    I also wrote that I returned at three, but that is still in the future. I meant that I had arranged to return then.
  10. Chris
    Record it as 5. The patient agreed to your offer, but the outcome of that future visit cannot be documented as complete now.
  11. Elena
    I was going to write anxious as the reason. But the patient only asked to rest. Should I leave out that interpretation unless I can support it?
  12. Chris
    Only add a supported 6 within your role, clearly distinguished from the facts. The account supports a request for rest, not an established finding of anxiety or another explanation.
  13. Elena
    The phrase refusing all care may already have been saved in the draft record. I should check its status rather than silently assume nobody could have seen it.
  14. Chris
    Follow the authorized 7 process if a saved entry needs correction. Keep the change traceable and do not obscure the original entry or its author.
  15. Elena
    So I should not delete the history merely to make the final record look as though the inaccurate wording never existed.
  16. Chris
    Preserve the 8 in the appropriate system. Transparent correction helps a later reader understand what was changed and why the current wording is more accurate.
  17. Elena
    The final note can state the quotation, the offer to return, and the agreement. It should not imply a formal decision about capacity or informed refusal.
  18. Chris
    That respects the 9. Those are distinct clinical and procedural matters, and the facts here do not establish that either assessment occurred.
  19. Elena
    I will record the request and agreed return time now. After the visit at three, I can document what happened rather than writing the outcome ahead.
  20. Chris
    Good. 10 can remain respectful without losing detail. The next clinician should understand the specific request and follow-up, rather than inherit a label about the patient.

Answers and explanations
  1. behavioral description Turn 2. A behavioral description identifies what was said or done instead of relying on a judgmental label.
  2. direct quotation Turn 4. The direct quotation preserves the patient's actual words and the limited request they express.
  3. template text Turn 6. Template text is preset wording that must still match the facts of the individual interaction.
  4. event time Turn 8. Event time identifies when the patient interaction occurred, distinct from when the note was entered.
  5. agreed follow-up Turn 10. Agreed follow-up is the accepted future plan, not evidence that the later visit has occurred.
  6. clinical inference Turn 12. A clinical inference must be distinguished from observed facts and cannot be invented to explain the event.
  7. amendment Turn 14. An amendment corrects a record through the approved process without concealing the original history.
  8. audit trail Turn 16. The audit trail provides traceability of entries and changes, supporting reliable correction rather than concealment.
  9. documentation scope Turn 18. Documentation scope limits the entry to what the supplied evidence and relevant professional process actually support.
  10. Objective documentation Turn 20. Objective documentation records supported facts and attributed information without the unsupported label in the original draft.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: My draft says the patient was difficult and refusing all care. Reading it back, I can see that it does not explain what happened during the interaction.
  2. Chris: Start with a behavioral description. What did the patient actually say, what did you offer, and which part of care was discussed?
  3. Elena: At two twenty, the patient said, "I want to rest before washing." I offered to return at three, and the patient agreed to that time.
  4. Chris: That direct quotation is useful because it states the request precisely. It does not support a claim that every kind of care was refused.
  5. Elena: No other care was discussed. I should not make the note broader just because the original template gave me a short phrase about noncompliance.
  6. Chris: Correct. Review the template text rather than let it determine the event. A convenient field does not justify recording something that the interaction did not establish.
  7. Elena: I am entering the note at two forty-five. The conversation happened at two twenty, so those times describe different things even though they are close together.
  8. Chris: Preserve the event time and the actual entry time according to our documentation procedure. Do not make the entry appear to have been written earlier than it was.
  9. Elena: I also wrote that I returned at three, but that is still in the future. I meant that I had arranged to return then.
  10. Chris: Record it as agreed follow-up. The patient agreed to your offer, but the outcome of that future visit cannot be documented as complete now.
  11. Elena: I was going to write anxious as the reason. But the patient only asked to rest. Should I leave out that interpretation unless I can support it?
  12. Chris: Only add a supported clinical inference within your role, clearly distinguished from the facts. The account supports a request for rest, not an established finding of anxiety or another explanation.
  13. Elena: The phrase refusing all care may already have been saved in the draft record. I should check its status rather than silently assume nobody could have seen it.
  14. Chris: Follow the authorized amendment process if a saved entry needs correction. Keep the change traceable and do not obscure the original entry or its author.
  15. Elena: So I should not delete the history merely to make the final record look as though the inaccurate wording never existed.
  16. Chris: Preserve the audit trail in the appropriate system. Transparent correction helps a later reader understand what was changed and why the current wording is more accurate.
  17. Elena: The final note can state the quotation, the offer to return, and the agreement. It should not imply a formal decision about capacity or informed refusal.
  18. Chris: That respects the documentation scope. Those are distinct clinical and procedural matters, and the facts here do not establish that either assessment occurred.
  19. Elena: I will record the request and agreed return time now. After the visit at three, I can document what happened rather than writing the outcome ahead.
  20. Chris: Good. Objective documentation can remain respectful without losing detail. The next clinician should understand the specific request and follow-up, rather than inherit a label about the patient.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Replace difficult with an observed account

Fictional charting exercise: a draft note says difficult but includes no observed behavior or patient quotation.

  1. Clinical preceptor: This simulated note calls the patient difficult. What did you actually observe or hear?
  2. New nurse: The draft doesn't include that information, so I can't support the label with an objective finding.
  3. Clinical preceptor: Could you replace it with uncooperative and keep the rest of the note?
  4. New nurse: That would still be an interpretation without a described event or patient response.
  5. Clinical preceptor: What do you need before revising the charting exercise accurately and usefully?
  6. New nurse: The actual observation, relevant words, and any documented intervention, without inventing a behavioral example.
  7. Clinical preceptor: Good. A neutral description should help the reader understand the event rather than judge the person.
  8. New nurse: I'll ask for the missing source details and distinguish quotations from my own observations.
  9. Clinical preceptor: Also keep the effect of any intervention separate from what you hoped it would achieve.
  10. New nurse: Understood. I won't replace an unsupported label with a fabricated fact just to make the note look complete.
A request for more explanation is not refusal

Separate fictional documentation simulation: the patient said, 'Please explain that again before we continue'; no further response is supplied.

  1. Staff nurse: The simulated patient asked me to explain again before continuing. Can I chart refused?
  2. Nurse educator: That wording goes beyond the supplied statement. They requested another explanation, not necessarily refused.
  3. Staff nurse: Would quoting the actual sentence preserve the distinction more accurately in the record?
  4. Nurse educator: Yes. Identify it as the patient's statement and describe any actual subsequent action separately.
  5. Staff nurse: The exercise doesn't say whether I explained again or whether the patient then agreed.
  6. Nurse educator: Then neither outcome should appear as a completed intervention or patient response.
  7. Staff nurse: I'll keep the unknown sequence visible rather than supply a likely ending for the chart.
  8. Nurse educator: Good. Objective charting can contain uncertainty when the source information is incomplete.
  9. Staff nurse: I'll ask for the remaining simulation details before documenting anything beyond the quotation.
  10. Nurse educator: That preserves what was said without turning a communication request into an unsupported judgment about cooperation.
Observed later does not mean caused by the intervention

Separate fictional note review: an intervention is documented and a later patient response is recorded, but the relationship has not been assessed.

  1. Allied health clinician: My note says the intervention caused the later improvement. Is that supported by the sequence alone?
  2. Documentation mentor: The sequence establishes that one happened before the other, not necessarily why the response changed.
  3. Allied health clinician: Should I remove the patient response completely to avoid making a causal claim?
  4. Documentation mentor: No. Preserve the observed response and its timing, while qualifying any interpretation of its cause.
  5. Allied health clinician: The simulation gives no additional assessment, so I shouldn't invent a clinical explanation.
  6. Documentation mentor: Correct. Distinguish the recorded intervention, the objective finding, and any assessment actually made.
  7. Allied health clinician: Could I say the response was observed after the intervention without saying caused by?
  8. Documentation mentor: That is a more accurate temporal description, provided it matches the supplied record.
  9. Allied health clinician: I'll revise the causal wording and retain the source observations for the reader.
  10. Documentation mentor: Good. The note can be useful without claiming a relationship that this exercise hasn't established.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Replace the label with the observable facts
  1. Read the draft-review exchange. State 2:20 event, 2:45 entry, and 3 p.m. agreed return as three separate times.
  2. Swap roles. Replace the broad label with the supplied quotation, offer, and agreement; do not invent the later outcome.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A plan entered as an event

At 11 a.m., a note says "Education completed at noon." The only supplied fact is an agreement to return at noon for education. The noon visit has not occurred.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reviewer: "Noon is still in the ___."

Answer and explanation

C. future

At eleven, the agreed noon visit has not yet occurred.

2. Author: "The visit is ___, not completed."

Answer and explanation

B. planned

The supplied fact is an agreement to return, not a completed education session.

3. Reviewer: "Correct the statement through the approved ___."

Answer and explanation

B. process

An inaccurate entry should be corrected transparently under the applicable documentation procedure.

4. Author: "The later entry must reflect the actual ___."

Answer and explanation

D. outcome

The result of the visit can be documented only after it is known.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Lesson 06: Documentation and Charting
Original fictional case: In a documentation simulation, nurse Elena drafts "Patient difficult and refusing all care." At 2:20 p.m., the patient said, "I want to rest before washing." Elena offered to return at 3 p.m.; the patient agreed. No other care was discussed. Elena enters the note at 2:45 p.m. Supervisor Chris reviews its wording. No capacity assessment, diagnosis, or explanation beyond the quoted request is supplied.
Language workshop: Replace the label with the observable facts
Communication goal: Select an objective, attributed clinical note and distinguish event time, entry time, and a later correction.
Language guidance: The quoted request supports a preference about timing, not refusal of all care or a capacity finding. Record the offered return and the agreement. Do not document a planned action as completed before it occurs.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Describe what was said and what you observed. / The patient said, "I want to rest before washing." / No other care was discussed in this interaction. / I offered to return at 3 p.m. / The patient agreed to that return time. / The event occurred at 2:20; this entry is being made at 2:45. / The record does not establish why the patient wanted to rest beyond the stated request. / A planned return is not a completed visit. / That phrase came from an earlier note and needs verification. / Attribute the entry to the person making it. / Use the approved amendment process rather than conceal the earlier entry. / This interaction does not establish a capacity assessment. / Include the facts needed for the next person's follow-up. / Use the approved term where a shortened form could be misunderstood. / Does every statement have a source in the supplied facts? / Record the later return only after its actual outcome is known.
Vocabulary: objective documentation: Recording observable or verifiable information without unsupported judgment. / subjective report: Information about an experience described by the person. / direct quotation: The person's exact words identified as a quote. / behavioral description: A factual account of what a person said or did. / clinical inference: A professional interpretation distinguished from the underlying facts. / event time: The time the described action or observation occurred. / entry time: The time the documentation is entered. / late entry: A later record of an earlier event, identified according to local procedure. / addendum: Additional information appended to an existing record. / amendment: A documented correction or change made through the approved process. / audit trail: A record showing relevant entries, changes, authors, and times. / record integrity: The reliability and traceability of documented information. / copy-forward: Reusing earlier text in a later record. / template text: Preset wording inserted into a record. / autopopulated field: A field filled automatically from another source. / author attribution: Identification of the person responsible for an entry. / contemporaneous note: Documentation made at or close to the relevant event. / factual correction: A change that corrects an inaccurate statement. / care declined: A specific offered action that the person did not accept. / agreed follow-up: The next contact or action actually agreed with the person. / capacity assessment: A qualified assessment of decision-making ability under applicable criteria. / informed refusal: Declining an intervention after the relevant information and process. / approved abbreviation: A shortened form permitted and understood in the local documentation system. / documentation scope: The limits of what an entry can support from the available evidence.
Speaking task: Read the draft-review exchange. State 2:20 event, 2:45 entry, and 3 p.m. agreed return as three separate times. Swap roles. Replace the broad label with the supplied quotation, offer, and agreement; do not invent the later outcome. Read the corrected education transfer. Stress planned at noon, not completed at eleven.
Partner: Elena: Nurse / Chris: Supervisor
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Difficult Families and BoundariesAcknowledge the frustration; keep the boundary+

Your goal

Respond to a family member with empathy, accurate limits, and a concrete next contact without promising another clinician.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Acknowledge the frustration; keep the boundary

In a simulation, family member Anika says, "Nobody tells us anything," and asks nurse Luis to explain a test result. The patient has agreed to include Anika in relevant updates; that permission does not authorize Luis to invent an interpretation. Luis can contact the responsible clinician and return with a status update by 4 p.m. No clinician meeting time is confirmed. Anika is frustrated but is not making threats. Urgent clinical concerns would follow the appropriate separate route.

Anika: Family member / Luis: Nurse

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What permission is supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. The patient has agreed to include Anika in relevant updates.

The permission concerns relevant updates to Anika, not unlimited authority or disclosure.

2. What can Luis promise?

Answer and explanation

A. His own status update by 4 p.m.

The brief gives Luis control of his update, not the clinician's schedule or clinical conclusion.

3. How is Anika's behavior described?

Answer and explanation

B. Frustrated, without threats

The supplied facts identify frustration but explicitly state that no threats are being made.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Permission to include a relative in updates is not automatically authority to decide for the patient. A nurse can acknowledge frustration without confirming an unsupported allegation. A status update promises communication; a clinical explanation requires the appropriate professional involvement.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
family liaison
Communication connecting family members with the care team.coordinate family liaison
patient preference
The person's expressed choice relevant to their care or communication.respect patient preferences
permission to share
An appropriate basis allowing specified information to be communicated.verify permission to share
confidentiality
Protection of information from inappropriate access or disclosure.maintain confidentiality
authorized recipient
A person appropriately entitled to receive the relevant information.confirm the authorized recipient
professional boundary
A limit that keeps actions and communication within an appropriate role.maintain a professional boundary
scope of practice
The professional activities permitted by role, competence, and applicable rules.work within scope of practice
empathic acknowledgment
Recognition of a person's concern or experience without unsupported agreement.offer empathic acknowledgment
validation
Recognition that an expressed feeling or concern has been heard.provide appropriate validation
de-escalation
Communication or action intended to reduce tension and support safety.use de-escalation skills
active listening
Attentive listening that checks the speaker's meaning.demonstrate active listening
clarifying question
A question used to establish the specific concern or missing detail.ask a clarifying question
family meeting
An arranged discussion involving appropriate relatives and care professionals.request a family meeting
clinical explanation
Interpretation of clinical information by an appropriately qualified professional.arrange a clinical explanation
status update
Information about what has happened and what remains pending.provide a status update
update commitment
A specific promise to communicate again at an agreed point.honor the update commitment
named contact
The identified person responsible for the next communication.provide a named contact
expectation management
Clear communication about what can and cannot currently be promised.improve expectation management
complaint pathway
The designated route for raising and reviewing service concerns.explain the complaint pathway
shared decision-making
A process combining relevant evidence with the patient's values and preferences.support shared decision-making
substitute decision-maker
A person with applicable authority to decide in specified circumstances.verify substitute decision-maker authority
consent discussion
A relevant exchange supporting an appropriately informed decision.arrange a consent discussion
communication preference
A stated choice about how or with whom information is shared.record communication preferences
safety boundary
A limit or action needed to protect people from actual risk.apply an appropriate safety boundary

Frustration, assertiveness, threats, and violence are not interchangeable labels. Respond to the actual behavior and local safety procedures. This scenario supplies no threat; it calls for listening, accurate boundaries, and reliable follow-through rather than punitive language.

C Notice the language

Boundaries should still offer a way forward

A boundary can sound dismissive when it ends the conversation without an alternative. Acknowledge the specific concern, explain what you can do, and state the next contact. Do not describe every frustrated family member as aggressive or confuse a firm request with a threat.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the experience
You have been waiting for a clear explanation, and that is frustrating.
Clarify the concern
Which question has not yet been answered?
Confirm participation
The patient has agreed that you can be included in these updates.
Keep the role clear
I can explain the next step, but I cannot invent an interpretation of the result.
Arrange the right discussion
I will contact the responsible clinician about that question.
Promise what you control
I will return with a status update by 4 p.m.
Separate the appointment
Four is my update time, not a confirmed meeting with the clinician.
Avoid unsupported reassurance
I cannot tell you the result is normal from the information we have here.
Keep listening
Let me check that I have understood your main concern.
Avoid defensiveness
I want to address the missing explanation rather than argue about your frustration.
Clarify authority
Being included in updates does not by itself determine decision-making authority.
Name the next contact
I will be your contact for this promised update.
Explain delay honestly
The meeting time is still unconfirmed, and I will tell you what I have established.
Offer the formal route
I can explain how to raise the communication concern through the appropriate pathway.
Protect urgent response
A new urgent clinical concern needs the appropriate clinical route immediately.
Close with the commitment
I will update you by four even if the meeting time remains pending.
6 language and precision notes
I understand
Name what you understand rather than use the phrase as a substitute for listening.
Nobody
A frustrated absolute can be acknowledged without debating every previous contact.
I promise
Restrict the promise to an action you can actually control.
Normal
Do not use this as reassurance without an established clinical basis.
Included versus authorized to decide
These are different permissions and responsibilities.
Difficult family
Describe the actual communication issue rather than treating the label as an assessment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening best acknowledges the concern?

Answer and explanation

D. You have been waiting for a clear explanation; let me check the unanswered question and arrange the right contact.

The opening recognizes the missing explanation and moves toward an appropriate response without unsupported reassurance.

2. Anika asks, "Will the clinician be here at four?" What is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Four is my promised status update; a clinician meeting time is not yet confirmed.

The brief distinguishes Luis's own update from an unconfirmed clinician meeting.

3. What should Luis avoid inferring from permission to share updates?

Answer and explanation

D. That Anika automatically has full decision-making authority

Being included in communication does not itself confer authority to make every decision.

4. It is four and Luis has no confirmed clinician meeting time. Which update keeps his promise?

Answer and explanation

D. The meeting time is still unconfirmed; here is the status I established and the next communication step.

The promise was Luis's status update by four, not a booked meeting or completed interpretation. Pending arrangements do not cancel that communication commitment.

E Conversations

Acknowledge the frustration; keep the boundary

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Anika: Family member / Luis: Nurse

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reassurance
  • clear explanation
  • named contact
  • update time
  • commitment
  • responsible clinician
  • concern
  • decision-making authority
  • updates
  • status update
  1. Anika
    Nobody tells us anything. We keep hearing that someone will explain the result, but we still do not know who that person is or when we will hear.
  2. Luis
    You have been waiting for a 1, and I can hear how frustrating that is. Let me check which question you most need answered.
  3. Anika
    I want to know what the test result means for my father. I do not want another message saying that somebody has passed the request to somebody else.
  4. Luis
    The patient has agreed to include you in these 2. I can contact the responsible clinician with that question, but I should not invent a clinical interpretation.
  5. Anika
    Does that mean you cannot tell me anything? I am not asking for a lecture about whose job it is. I need a practical next step.
  6. Luis
    The next step is for me to contact the 3 and confirm the response arrangements. I will stay responsible for the update I promise you.
  7. Anika
    All right, but what exactly are you promising? Earlier I heard soon, and soon turned into several hours without a clear answer.
  8. Luis
    I will give you a 4 by four this afternoon. That will include what I have confirmed and anything still awaiting a response.
  9. Anika
    So the clinician will meet us at four? I can ask my sister to come then if that is a firm time.
  10. Luis
    Four is my 5, not a confirmed clinician meeting. I do not want you to rearrange the family around an appointment that has not been agreed.
  11. Anika
    Thank you for making that distinction. I am still worried. Can you at least say the result is probably normal because nobody has come urgently?
  12. Luis
    I cannot give that 6 from silence or timing. The result needs the appropriate clinical explanation, and I will carry that specific question to the responsible team.
  13. Anika
    Please tell them how hard the silence has been. I should not have to keep chasing before someone explains what is happening.
  14. Luis
    I will include that 7 accurately. If you want it reviewed through the formal communication-complaint route, I can explain that process as well.
  15. Anika
    I would like the explanation arranged first. Since my father agreed to include me, does that also mean I can make all the decisions for him?
  16. Luis
    Inclusion in updates does not by itself establish 8. His preferences and the applicable decision process still matter. We should clarify any specific decision with the appropriate team.
  17. Anika
    For now, I need the result discussion arranged and one reliable contact. Please pass the history on so I do not have to explain it again next shift.
  18. Luis
    I will be the 9 for this promised update and document the concern and next step appropriately. The team should receive the question you have already explained.
  19. Anika
    If the meeting time is still unknown at four, please tell me that directly. Waiting without knowing whether the request was received is the hardest part.
  20. Luis
    I will keep that 10. You will hear the current status from me by four, with the next communication step, rather than an invented answer or an unconfirmed appointment.

Answers and explanations
  1. clear explanation Turn 2. Clear explanation identifies the unmet communication need Luis is acknowledging rather than dismissing.
  2. updates Turn 4. Updates are the relevant communications the patient has permitted Anika to receive in this case.
  3. responsible clinician Turn 6. The responsible clinician is the appropriate contact for the clinical explanation sought in this scenario.
  4. status update Turn 8. A status update reports what has been established and what remains pending, not necessarily a completed clinical explanation.
  5. update time Turn 10. Update time identifies Luis's own communication commitment, not a clinician meeting that has not been arranged.
  6. reassurance Turn 12. Reassurance about a normal result would lack a clinical basis in the facts supplied here.
  7. concern Turn 14. Concern refers to Anika's complaint about the communication experience, which can be passed on accurately.
  8. decision-making authority Turn 16. Decision-making authority is distinct from permission to receive relevant information and requires the applicable basis.
  9. named contact Turn 18. The named contact is the identified person accountable for this promised follow-up communication.
  10. commitment Turn 20. Commitment refers to the promised update Luis can control even if the meeting remains unconfirmed.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Responding to nobody tells us anything

Fictional family-communication rehearsal between a nurse and a patient liaison: a family member has said nobody tells us anything; sharing permissions are unconfirmed.

  1. Patient liaison: The family says nobody tells us anything. How will you respond without becoming defensive?
  2. Registered nurse: I'll acknowledge their frustration and explain that I can help arrange an appropriate conversation.
  3. Patient liaison: Would you promise to disclose the full clinical record to repair the communication problem?
  4. Registered nurse: No. I need to respect privacy and confirm what information can appropriately be shared.
  5. Patient liaison: How can you explain that boundary without sounding as though you're refusing to help?
  6. Registered nurse: I can help identify the right clinician and arrange a discussion within the applicable sharing arrangements.
  7. Patient liaison: The family may ask you for a decision outside your scope of practice.
  8. Registered nurse: I'll explain my role clearly and refer that question to the responsible clinician rather than guess.
  9. Patient liaison: Please avoid promising a family meeting time before the participants are available.
  10. Registered nurse: Agreed. I'll take responsibility for requesting the conversation and returning a confirmed arrangement.
Correcting an unconfirmed family-meeting time

Separate fictional communication case: a nurse said the family meeting would be at two, but clinician availability has not been confirmed.

  1. Nurse coordinator: I told the family two o'clock, but I hadn't confirmed the clinician's availability.
  2. Social worker: We need to correct that promise directly rather than leave them waiting for an unconfirmed meeting.
  3. Nurse coordinator: I'll say I gave a time before confirming it, and I'm sorry for that confusion.
  4. Social worker: Good. What can you offer now without creating another unsupported promise?
  5. Nurse coordinator: I can request a confirmed time and remain the contact for the scheduling update.
  6. Social worker: Should the correction also clarify the purpose of the proposed family meeting?
  7. Nurse coordinator: Yes, within the agreed scope and privacy arrangements, without promising a specific clinical decision.
  8. Social worker: I'll help check who needs to attend and what questions the family wants addressed.
  9. Nurse coordinator: I'll distinguish collecting their questions from guaranteeing that every requested outcome can be provided.
  10. Social worker: Then we'll return with a verified arrangement and a clear account of who will address which concerns.
An apology without crossing a privacy boundary

Separate fictional liaison call: an earlier message implied any relative could receive updates, but the appropriate sharing permissions have not been verified.

  1. Patient liaison: Our earlier message suggested any relative could receive clinical updates. That needs correction.
  2. Charge nurse: I'll acknowledge the misleading wording and explain that appropriate sharing permissions must be checked.
  3. Patient liaison: Can you make that correction without implying the relative has done something wrong?
  4. Charge nurse: Yes. The problem was our explanation; the check is about information sharing, not a judgment about them.
  5. Patient liaison: What should happen if they ask you to confirm details while the check is pending?
  6. Charge nurse: I'll maintain the privacy boundary and help them reach the appropriate conversation route.
  7. Patient liaison: Could you promise that permission will be granted once the form is reviewed?
  8. Charge nurse: No. Review is not a guaranteed outcome, and I shouldn't imply authority I don't have.
  9. Patient liaison: I'll support the follow-up and make sure the family receives a clear explanation of the next step.
  10. Charge nurse: Thank you. We can repair the message and remain helpful without making an unverified disclosure.

An update is not a meeting

A therapist promises a family a 5 p.m. update about arranging a care conference. The conference time remains unconfirmed. The patient has agreed to the relevant family involvement.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Family member: "Is five the conference ___?"

Answer and explanation

D. time

The question asks whether the update time also establishes a conference appointment.

2. Therapist: "Five is my ___; the conference is not yet booked."

Answer and explanation

C. update

The only confirmed promise concerns the therapist's communication.

3. Family member: "Please explain the current ___ even if it is pending."

Answer and explanation

D. status

The family requests accurate information about arrangements, not an invented booking.

4. Therapist: "I will keep my communication ___."

Answer and explanation

B. commitment

The update can still be delivered even when another person's meeting is unconfirmed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 07: Difficult Families and Boundaries
Original fictional case: In a simulation, family member Anika says, "Nobody tells us anything," and asks nurse Luis to explain a test result. The patient has agreed to include Anika in relevant updates; that permission does not authorize Luis to invent an interpretation. Luis can contact the responsible clinician and return with a status update by 4 p.m. No clinician meeting time is confirmed. Anika is frustrated but is not making threats. Urgent clinical concerns would follow the appropriate separate route.
Language workshop: Acknowledge the frustration; keep the boundary
Communication goal: Respond to a family member with empathy, accurate limits, and a concrete next contact without promising another clinician.
Language guidance: Permission to include a relative in updates is not automatically authority to decide for the patient. A nurse can acknowledge frustration without confirming an unsupported allegation. A status update promises communication; a clinical explanation requires the appropriate professional involvement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You have been waiting for a clear explanation, and that is frustrating. / Which question has not yet been answered? / The patient has agreed that you can be included in these updates. / I can explain the next step, but I cannot invent an interpretation of the result. / I will contact the responsible clinician about that question. / I will return with a status update by 4 p.m. / Four is my update time, not a confirmed meeting with the clinician. / I cannot tell you the result is normal from the information we have here. / Let me check that I have understood your main concern. / I want to address the missing explanation rather than argue about your frustration. / Being included in updates does not by itself determine decision-making authority. / I will be your contact for this promised update. / The meeting time is still unconfirmed, and I will tell you what I have established. / I can explain how to raise the communication concern through the appropriate pathway. / A new urgent clinical concern needs the appropriate clinical route immediately. / I will update you by four even if the meeting time remains pending.
Vocabulary: family liaison: Communication connecting family members with the care team. / patient preference: The person's expressed choice relevant to their care or communication. / permission to share: An appropriate basis allowing specified information to be communicated. / confidentiality: Protection of information from inappropriate access or disclosure. / authorized recipient: A person appropriately entitled to receive the relevant information. / professional boundary: A limit that keeps actions and communication within an appropriate role. / scope of practice: The professional activities permitted by role, competence, and applicable rules. / empathic acknowledgment: Recognition of a person's concern or experience without unsupported agreement. / validation: Recognition that an expressed feeling or concern has been heard. / de-escalation: Communication or action intended to reduce tension and support safety. / active listening: Attentive listening that checks the speaker's meaning. / clarifying question: A question used to establish the specific concern or missing detail. / family meeting: An arranged discussion involving appropriate relatives and care professionals. / clinical explanation: Interpretation of clinical information by an appropriately qualified professional. / status update: Information about what has happened and what remains pending. / update commitment: A specific promise to communicate again at an agreed point. / named contact: The identified person responsible for the next communication. / expectation management: Clear communication about what can and cannot currently be promised. / complaint pathway: The designated route for raising and reviewing service concerns. / shared decision-making: A process combining relevant evidence with the patient's values and preferences. / substitute decision-maker: A person with applicable authority to decide in specified circumstances. / consent discussion: A relevant exchange supporting an appropriately informed decision. / communication preference: A stated choice about how or with whom information is shared. / safety boundary: A limit or action needed to protect people from actual risk.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.
